ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28370	Thank you, Mr. President of the Parliament. Dear ladies and gentlemen of Parliament. Here are three laws, energy laws that are very important and above all, which are changes to the energy law and new laws on electricity triangulation and the law on regulation of energy activities. Refort energy sector in the Republic of Croatia began in July 2001 when the Croatian Parliament adopted the concept of reform adopted a package of energy laws. This enables, among other things, the creation of an institutional energy sector framework with particular emphasis on energy market liberalisation. The five Directives of the European Union that came into force after 2001 are newly regulating issues in the energy sector. The additional reason for amending these laws is also the difficulties in implementing the energy sector reform under existing laws. It is necessary to redesign an existing system and market operator by setting up a transmission system operator to perform integrated power system management and power transmission functions. It is necessary to separate the market operator from HEP. Such a solution brings unique responsibility for the security of the electricity system and is in line with solutions in most European countries. Further opening of the market requires a more operational energy regulator. So short a few sentences about each of these laws. Amendments to the Energy Act. The energy law is a roof law for all laws in the energy sector and provides a comprehensive legal framework for the sector. This law sets out, first and foremost, the structure of regulated and free energy prices. Methodology for the determination of tariff items with a height of headings. And jurisdiction over its adoption which is reasonably assigned to the Government, the Ministry of Regulatory and Energy Entities to which a particular tariff system is concerned. The law also accurately formulate general conditions for energy supply and consumer protection measures. To speed up solving the problem of illegal construction, these amendments to the law prohibit the collection of buildings with a construction permit on energy networks. The electricity market law lays down regulated and marketable electricity activities. How to obtain the status of a privileged producer, and the right to encourage. As well as the procedures for granting approval for the construction of new power plants, and the publication and execution of a public tender if access is to be opened for that construction. The law lays down the responsibilities and responsibilities of transmission operator and distribution system operator. AND their independence in a vertically integrated company. The independence of electricity loads from electricity transmission and distribution activities and the obligation of all suppliers to assume electricity from renewable energy sources and generation as well as the obligation of public information providers is regulated. Special emphasis is on the protection of customers from the category of households and small customers. Additional roles of market operators in the performance of operations, e.g. the calculation of balancing energy, and the collection and distribution of funds for the promotion of renewable energy sources and cogeneration have been established. The annual spending limit has been reduced to obtaining preferential customer status, from 40 to 20 gigawatt hours, and dynamics have been established until the market is finally opened. The public service obligation of supply and the rights and obligations of privileged and tariff buyers has been established in a transparent manner. The transitional and final provisions of the law lay down the obligations of HEP for the provision of public services and for the establishment of a new structure, in particular the separation of supply and distribution. Issuing of market operators from HEP and public service obligations of customers who are left without suppliers or who do not wish to use the status of a privileged buyer. In order to establish and implement the regulation of energy activities, the system of regulated action of energy subjects, and the establishment and arrangement of the energy market by the new energy regulation law, it is proposed to establish an independent, independent, non-profit, public institutions called the Croatian Energy Regulatory Agency. The founder of the agency is proposed that the Republic of Croatia, the agency for its work, corresponds to the Croatian Assembly, this law regulates the internal structure of the agency, accurately determines and determines its business and activities as an activity of special state interest. For example, these are the tasks of disposing of permits to carry out energy activities. By providing methodologies and supervision of the application of tariff systems. Control of the work of energy subjects. All of the so far, this is important to say, the acts of the Council for the Regulatory of Energy Activities and all the solutions of that Council and the permits are valid for the time of the one issued, so it simply ensures the continuity of the current council and the new agency. The reasons why this law is important and important to adopt already and this year are not only a alignment with the European Union directives but also our international obligations and the need to be members of European associations so that we can use the funds, that we are moving towards the liberalisation of the energy sector as quickly as possible. To regulate the position of renewable energy sources and cogenerations that have not been legally regulated so far. In view of the need to establish a level of operationality in the work of regulators and normal compliance with the way energy law, electricity market law and energy regulation law. Therefore, I ask you to adopt these amendments to the law on energy and new laws on the electricity market and the law on energy regulation. Thank you.

ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28374	Your Honor, Mr. President, the Honorable Minister, the Honorable Representatives. The Committee on the Economy, Development and Reconstruction of the Croatian Parliament at the 21st session held on 29 November 2004 discussed the proposal for a law amending and supplementing the law on energy with the Final Proposal of the Law, the proposal for a regulation of energy activities with the Final Proposal for a Law and the Proposal for a Law on the Market of Electricity with the Final Proposal for Law, which was submitted by the Government of the Republic of Croatia by the Act of 18 November 2004, with a proposal to comply with Article 159. The Rules of Procedure of the Croatian Parliament of Laws shall be adopted by an urgent procedure. The Committee shall be within the meaning of Article 67. The Rules of Procedure of the Croatian Parliament discussed the above-mentioned Bills as a parent working body. On a proposal from the Committee president, the Committee discussed the proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Energy Act with the Final Proposal for Law, the emergency procedure, the 1st and 2nd reading, the proposal for a law on the electricity market with the final draft law, the emergency procedure, the 1st and 2nd reading and the proposal for a law on energy regulation with the final draft law, the emergency procedure 1st and 2nd reading. In his opening statement, the applicant's representative pointed out that the regulatory energy activity process started in 2001 and that, after a short delay in these legislative proposals, the process continued. When drawing up these legislative proposals, binding guidelines of the European Union have been taken into account, in particular those 5 relating to these legislative proposals. The applicant specifically pointed out that according to the European Union directives, the energy system should be guided in a way that enables basic decisions to be made in one place. These legislative proposals prescribe guidelines for other laws to be passed and are related to energy activity. A request for a structured energy price has also been introduced, so that all consumers know what enters that price. Particularly protected are natural persons and small businesses, as energy users. It is prescribed that all users have the right to choose to buy electricity. The proposing representative also stressed the jurisdiction of the Croatian regulatory agency whose decisions are final, which means that only judicial protection can be sought against them. These legislative proposals have established a new way of calculating the transmission of electricity abroad. The adoption of these laws is a presumption of the adoption of sublegal acts in the field of energy which should result in greater liberalisation of the energy market. In the opening of the debate, the Committee President met the members of the Committee with the material of the Croatian Chamber of Commerce whose representative pointed out that HEP supports the adoption of the proposed laws, while INA and JANAF have objections which are united in that material. The basic observation by INA and JANAF is that, in view of the importance of the matter regulated by these legislative proposals, they should go into the regular procedure of adoption without the application of an emergency procedure. The representative of the Croatian Chamber of Commerce has proposed that the fundamental law relating to the harmonisation of Croatian legislation with the legislation of the European Union should also be discussed in regular procedure. The president of the trade union in Croatia's electricity industry stressed that these legal proposals begin the abolition of the HEP monopoly and continue the process of restructuring of HEP. Following the discussion, the Committee unanimously reached the next conclusion. It is supported by the adoption of the Law amending and supplementing the Energy Act, the adoption of the Energy Regulation Act and the adoption of the Electricity Market Act. Thank you.
ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28375	Thank you very much, Mr. President. I'm opening a club debate. Croatian Democratic Community Representatives' Club, respected representative Kresimir Cosic. Here you go.
ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28376	Your Honor, Mr. President, Mr. Secretary, ladies and gentlemen. The 2001 Energy Act, as known, is a roof law for all other laws in the field of energy. It was prepared nearly 5 years ago, and it is understandable today that it is not in line with the new European Union directives adopted in 2003 and there are legitimate reasons for updating it. Because of this, it is not enough framework for all other laws in the energy sector and the efforts of the Government of Croatia to modernise and update it have been fully justified and therefore here today we are discussing this package of laws. I'll just point out some shortcomings in this law. The current Energy Act provides that energy activities are separate from the transmission of electricity and the management of the electricity system. That's not good for many reasons. Not only because of the fact that this does not correspond to European knowledge today, but simply the necessary level of synergy and uniform management of the electricity system management system and the transmission system enables far greater efficiency of the system as a whole. Furthermore, Directive 203 54 EC does not know a separate system operator from an energy transmission operator, but only a single transmission system operator, tsl TSO transmission system operator to be responsible for the following tasks: ensuring the long-term capacity of the demand system, contributing to security of supply, managing energy flows in the network, informing the operator of adjacent networks, non-discrimination of network users, informing network users etc. In addition, there are additional reasons why it was necessary to update the existing energy law. A very significant problem of rationalising energy consumption, i.e. The increase in energy efficiency is not sufficiently defined and regulated by the current Energy Act and no compliance with the European Union directives has been achieved. The relevant law further does not adequately and sufficiently clearly describe the tariff systems and the procedures for their adoption for certain energy activities. Furthermore, the applicable gas economy law does not cover the energy activity of natural gas supply. In addition, natural gas supply and natural gas distribution activities as public services do not have a regulated price tariff methodology. Furthermore, as regards oil and petroleum products, the applicable law does not include the energy activities of biofuel production, oil pipeline transport, oil and oil transport, and oil derivatives and biofuels by road vehicles. This is due to the fact that the energy operators present cannot obtain authorisations to carry out the activities for which they are otherwise qualified. Furthermore, the adoption of this law and all the laws that we are discussing today, which are related to this law, will result in the opening of the energy market according to the European Union directives and will contribute to the rapid liberalisation of the energy sector and the rationalisation of energy entities in the Republic of Croatia. I will stress only some important elements relating to harmonisation with European Union directives and documents, the adoption of common rules on the electricity market, the adoption of common rules on the internal market for natural gas, the adoption and promotion of the use of electricity from renewable energy sources, the promotion of cogeneration based on the use of useful heat and the promotion of the use of biofuels and other renewable fuels in transport. So from all that's been said, it's quite clear that for the past five years they've made important changes in the existing Energy Act. So the main objective of this bill is to better identify and regulate all energy activities in the Republic of Croatia, ensure a unique view of all energy activities, a single management and management system with all the important parts of this complex energy system in the Republic of Croatia. Establish a more comprehensive legal framework for other energy sector laws in order to make production, transfer, distribution and supply of energy to all customers in the Republic of Croatia more reliable and efficient. In addition, all documents place particular emphasis on environmental protection measures, renewable energy, cogeneration and energy efficiency. Now allow me a few words about the proposal for a law on energy regulation. Recently in this House, we discussed the Council's annual report on energy regulation. We took note of it, with certain observations which were primarily related to the fact that the Energy Regulatory Council has, in accordance with the existing law, significant means to charge for the provision and authorisation of activities in the energy sector. However, this Energy Regulatory Council had only three employees with such significant revenues and I personally suggested that we be provided with a report on the financial operations of the Energy Regulatory Council, but here we are today discussing a proposal for a law on the regulation of energy activities which, in some ways, dodging or abolishing the Energy Regulatory Council, which really makes sense and justification, I would like to say a few words. The Republic of Croatia is a signator of the European Energy Charter, which has been established to accelerate the liberalisation of the energy sector and allow third parties to access existing transmission and transport systems of energy in the Republic of Croatia. The aim of opening the market is to give all energy suppliers an equal opportunity to supply customers with objectively visible and impartial conditions, and the establishment of regulation should allow for control of the achievement of the objectives set. The proposed law, as I said about regulation of energy activities, establishes and this is highlighted in the opening section, the Agency for the Regulatory of Energy Activities as a public and independent institution that is structured as follows. She has her own board of directors, she needs to have her own board president, she has an agency, i.e. staff of the Agency and she should have a director as an appropriate professional. The Administrative Board of the Energy Regulatory Agency shall be appointed by the Croatian Parliament. I hope that we will have this very soon in the Croatian Parliament in the procedure, and the director is elected by the administrative council as an expert in the public competition. The Statute of the Agency shall be adopted by the Administrative Board of the Agency, with the prior consent of the Government of the Republic of Croatia. The activities of the Agency shall be of particular interest to the Republic of Croatia and shall be carried out by the Agency on the basis of public authority. That's quite understandable from everything that's been said so far. Any acts for which the adoption is solely competent, shall be adopted by the Administrative Board of the Agency on a proposal from the Director of the Agency. The President of the Governing Council shall manage the work of the Agency, represent and represent the Agency, and shall organise expert work to prepare all proposals to the Governing Council. The annual report on the overall situation in the energy sector, as well as the financial report, on the implementation of the financial plan, shall be submitted by the Agency to the Croatian Parliament and the Government of the Republic of Croatia. I stress and repeat the existing structure of the Energy Regulatory Council, according to which it consists of only five members standing here, I said three, disabling its independent and active performance in the field of energy regulation and we really had an important institution that was actually fictitious, but as the virtual institution says today, because such important responsibilities, the responsibilities of the activities of the institution with only a few employees could not be carried out. How did she do those jobs? They were carried out in the following way, that most of them, or almost all of the professional tasks for the purposes of the Council, were carried out by the Energy Institute "Croatian Fire" as an institution over which the Council had no control. In such circumstances, the Council does not have the necessary conditions or has not had the necessary conditions to exercise its functions independently of the regulatory body in the field of energy regulation and this has led to difficulties in carrying out energy activities and the establishment of energy markets with the potential for long-term serious consequences, so such a Council work organisation had to be abolished and this is what we are likely to vote on Friday and adopt. It should, in particular, be pointed out once again that the Agency for the Regulatory of Energy Activities in the scope of its affairs, by jurisdiction, is stronger than many ministries in the Republic of Croatia. I usually make fun of myself, it's practically one strong ministry, the Ministry of Energy, but for reasons of simpler management at the government level, that institution has a title as agreed and adopted, i.e. The Agency for the Regulatory of Energy Activities. Justifying such an important institution with this law is only the first step. Functioning, especially the quality functioning of this Agency is a job that will take a long time, and the first step is extremely important, i.e. the installation of quality people in view of the complexity of the tasks for which the Agency will be competent, especially the leaders. A good law doesn't have to be good in implementation. Without quality good people capable of coping with all these challenges, the Agency will not be able to meet the challenges and I hope that this issue, which is just as important as the law itself, will pay special attention. Furthermore, a few words on the proposal for a law on the electricity market. As is known, by implementing the provisions of the Electricity Market Act, and by respecting the provisions of the Energy Act, on 1 July 2002, Croatia's electricity industry was transformed into a system of related societies of basic and accompanying activities. Respecting the provisions of the Law on Commercial Companies, Croatia's electricity, as is known, is organized as a concept called HEP Group, run by the ruling company Croatia's electricity industry, and all the others below are listed companies over which Croatia's electricity has a dominant influence. It is HEP production, HEP transmission, HEP distribution and HEP supply. The adoption of the Energy Act, the Law on the Regulatory of Energy Activities and the Law on the Market in Electricity practically emanates one serious and large process of restructuring the HEP. What reasons for the restructuring of HEP according to this Law have already been said and I just want to point out once again. In particular, it is a significant unsustainable alignment with the requirements of Directive 2003-54-EC. As a result, this Law will introduce, along with a single transmission system operator, what we have said, the integration of transmission and management systems, the introduction of a distribution system operator, as well as a market operator. This is the important element since today's market operator is in HEP structure and this is in line with the European Union directives, in line with the requirements on the liberalisation of the energy market, is not acceptable. Therefore, there are very complex and important structural changes ahead of HEP for all of these reasons. Finally, such a solution as is known is a European standard. The establishment of the so-called The TSO operator is the task ahead. First of all, it would point out, especially and important because of the international obligations of the Republic of Croatia, i.e. because of the positioning of the formal and strategic Croatian transmission system operator in international frameworks. There is a near signing of important international contracts, i.e., accession to the European Association of transmission system operators, so-called ETSO. And positioning of the Croatian transmission system operator in the bodies of the European Electricity Transfer Association, UCTE. Finally, for all these reasons, the Croatian Democratic Community Club strongly supports this great effort by the Government of the Republic of Croatia to modernise the energy sector of the Republic of Croatia as a whole. And as I said, the first step in modernising this energy sector, this energy system is to adopt a certain package of laws. Again, I stress, in the end, the implementation and implementation of these laws is, I would say, far more complex tasks and more responsible than the definition of laws and their adoption. And for this reason, the Croatian Democratic Community Club expects the implementation of all these laws to be made as high-quality, organised and efficient as this first step towards modernising the legislative system of the Republic of Croatia in the energy sector. Thank you very much.

However, in the Republic of Croatia everything was in the hands of the Energy Regulatory Council, which actually consists of 5 members. And for those five members of this Energy Regulatory Council, there was no sophisticated professional service, but all the professional support for their decisions was provided by the Institute of Fire. So, whether it is electricity or gas tariffs, the restructuring of INE and HEP, the planning of development, etc., all decisions were subject to the famous professional influence of the Institute "Croatia Pozar" and its director, the famous strategy of Goran Granić, and of course not for free, but equally not necessarily professionally and impartially and of course not necessarily in the national interest of Croatia. More specifically, without excess environments, the whole system has been regulated by law so far that the director of the "Croatian Fire" Institute was actually the master of Croatia's energy sector. It is therefore logical to establish a private agency with the Council as its professional service when it is already going to create an independent regulator, a liberal energy market. So why all the rush to liberalising the energy sector when no one in Croatia knows exactly how much electricity our country will need in 5 or 25 years. How much energy will we be able to recover from our investments, for example in Serbia, Bosnia and Herzegovina? No national development strategy has yet been developed. The alleged reason for the reform of the energy sector, i.e. the law on the liberalisation of the electricity market, which was discussed by the Parliament in 2000, was Croatia's involvement in European integration. But that's certainly completely pointless. Why? In particular, the 1997 European Union Directive on the liberalisation of the electricity market advocates national sovereignty over its own energy and transport systems, i.e. power lines, pipelines, pipelines, etc. In addition, the said directive provides for 2 equal underlines the equal model of regulating electricity markets, so on. The open market model, which was in fact the basis of that 2000 strategy. Based on the example of part of the EU member states and even more famous California. In other words, from the other old model of the only buyer that is in power in Croatia today. And they apply it in the European Union, including Italy, France and Ireland. So, if the Italians, French and Irish, along with this system of the only buyer, recorded economic growth within the European Union, why could it not continue to apply to Croatia? At that time, however, the government chose an open market model that envisioned the development of INA and HEP to private private companies that freely, therefore without state control, trade in energy and fuel. However, Croatia's national interest is to monitor its market energy in the model of the only buyer. Not even the most concerned supporters of the free market in the European Union or in the United States would allow themselves, for example, to be monitored by 75 to 80% of the energy market. The privatisation of HEP also meant the sale of Croatian family silver. In addition, newly created private companies will not have the conditions to survive, so they will gladly increase electricity prices differently in different parts of Croatia. The fight of private profit companies will eliminate their will to expand power plants and improve the transport network, and contrary to the current obligation of HEP to provide electricity to everyone in the territory of Croatia, this new model of the open market involves consumer freedom to choose the most favourable energy supplier, even outside Croatia. the freedom to import electricity, for example from Ukraine. But at the same time, the freedom of energy suppliers to choose a buyer of electricity outside Croatia, for example, in Italy. No matter how much Croatia really needs energy right now. So none of the privates in that model are responsible for, for example, the lack of electricity, because they actually aim at profit, i.e. exporting electricity where it can be sold without risk. All of these happened in California. After privatising and liberalising the electricity market according to the open market model and causing energy supply instability and power reductions, watch out for power cuts in California. This, however, would cause even more suffering and power reduction in Croatia than those we witnessed in 92. On the other hand, a sharp rise in electricity and fuel prices, which would completely affect the Croatian economy. Although work is fortunately far behind us, Croatia has no reason to repeat California's mistakes today. All Croatian residents with discomfort remember periods of power cuts during the war years. It is possible, however, that we will experience a reduction of electricity throughout Croatia in a few years, for example. For those who do not consider it possible, it is worth mentioning the news of the CCN as of 18 January this year in connection with the power shortage in California. So, in California with such a successful economy that it would belong to the eight richest countries in the world if, by any chance, it was independent, in January of this year even the famous Governor Schwarzenegger and his government were tortured by power shortages. California was missing 18 gigawatts a day, so just before the summer heats when the air conditioning device greatly increases energy consumption. The crisis hasn't been resolved until today. The state of California imported some electricity from other countries, and it paid off part of the debt only to the largest private distributor, a private electricity distributor in California, which is "Pacific GS and eletic", which, after all of that, was bankrupt. The governor proposed austerity measures for higher electricity consumption and increased electricity prices by 150 to 300%. Sounds very familiar to you, 'cause so? So the whole thing is out in California to the citizens, who are the least guilty of it. Of course, far from me wanting to compare Croatia with California, which is no longer necessary to doubt whether such a vital economy is going to know how to get out of this energy crisis, especially since Governor Arnold Schwarzenegger is. Yet if California could afford so much trouble from the so-called. The liberalisation and model of the open electricity market, what would happen to Croatia then? That's why we at the HSP Club repeat what we said in 2000. It must leave the open market model urgently and return to the only buyer model, such as Italy or as such a model in the European Union has France, Ireland and some other countries. And secondly, don't forget that with this package of laws here today, the proposed law on the privatisation of HEP has to be changed, otherwise nothing will be done, this will remain a dead letter on paper. Unfortunately, we lost most of our INA. Therefore, leaving the open market model and changing the HEP Privatisation Act would finally protect the Croatian electricity system from the risk of collapse and protect the Croatian economy from possible collapse. The only damage could then be suffered by Croatian Energy Strategy Goran Granic due to the decline in the value of the shares of the Institute of Hrvoje Pozar, but I think it is a damage that we can all bear. The energy regulation law of Articles 6 and 7 defines the establishment of the Croatian Energy Regulatory Agency HERA as an independent non-profit organisation to regulate participants and relations in the entire energy market of Croatia. This is certainly a better solution than this current ineffective regulatory council that does not have its own logistics and has not been able to fulfil the expected tasks. ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28442	Thank you very much, Mr. President, ladies and gentlemen of the Parliament. This proposed supplement of the Fire Department creates the basis for further funding of decentralised fire functions by the State towards local self-government units. By 31 December of this year, Article 58 shall apply. A law on fire that came into force in 2000 when units of local self-government took over funding for public fire departments. This Article 58 also provided for the dynamics of the acquisition of full financing of public fire departments, thus providing that in 2002 the founders' cities would assume funding of 25%, 2003. 50%, 2004. 75% and from 1.1 next year 100%. Since practice has shown that the fiscal and financial capacities of the founders of public fire departments are not sufficient for regular financing, last year the government passed last year an amendment to the Income Tax Act in September, where 1% of the income tax is left to local self-government units to finance these decentralised functions. However, these funds were not sufficient either. So there was to be a basis for further financing of public professional fire departments and next year at the level of funds allocated this year. This is exactly what this article in the Law on Amendments and Supplements of the Fire Department provides. It continues to oblige the government to finance public professional fire departments in the following year at the level of funds this year. By also taking over the obligation to adopt a minimum financial standard for the implementation of these decentralised fire departments, which would definitely resolve the problem of financing fire departments, and yet in accordance with the Law on the Protection and Rescue and Action of the new Protection and Rescue Authority. So, once again just briefly, this Law creates the assumption that the Government of the State Budget continues to finance the action of professional public fire departments at the level of funds that were this year. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28454	Your Honor, Mr. President, Secretary of State. The Fire Department has been decentralised so that the fire protection obligation is restored from state level to local self-government. By doing so, units of local self-government have been given the right and obligations to establish as well as to finance the fire department. Article 58 of the Fire Department lays down a transitional provision by which the financing of the regular activity of public fire departments is ensured from the state budget by gradually taking over the financing obligations of local self-government units and, in fact, from 1. 1. In 2005, the obligation to finance became an exclusive obligation for cities and municipalities. However, the fact is that the majority of units of local self-government due to insufficient economic development and scarce financial resources have budgets from which they are simply unable to finance all the obligations undertaken by the decentralisation process, thus not being able to finance fire protection either. They are not able to play out sufficient financial resources for the normal financing of fire departments whose founders are. According to available data, only five cities of the founders of public fire departments of 58 of them can fulfil the obligation to finance fire protection. Furthermore, the recently adopted Income Tax Act raises an untaxable part of the base, which ultimately means less income from local self-government units to finance public needs in its field and so on by fire. And all of this further reduces their modest opportunities to allocate the necessary funds anyway. Obviously aware of the problem that has existed so far, or which so far exists in the financing and functioning of fire protection, which since 1.1.2005 and further worsened, the Government has tried to bridge the proposal for a law supplementing the Fire Department that has been tried before us by the above problems. However, these additions only propose setting out the government's obligations and powers to make a decision on a minimum financial standard to carry out the activities of public fire departments which will be the basis for planning aid from the state budget. I believe that this is a temporary solution that will ultimately not solve the existing problems completely. These changes only partially resolved the problem of fire financing because the concession of part of the income tax applied only to units of local self-government that were founders of public fire departments. This solution that is still in effect is very bad for two reasons. The reduction in income tax does not apply to all units of local self-government but only to cities and municipalities that are founders of public fire departments, and secondly in most of these cities 1% of income tax is not nearly enough to finance the regular activity of public fire departments. The fact that the separation of part of income tax applies only to units of local self-government which are founders of public fire departments is not respected by the provisions of Article 134. The Constitution of the Republic of Croatia and the provisions of Article 19 The law on local and regional, regional self-government under which units of local self-government carry out matters of local importance among other things and fire protection in their own self-government. So small and large, the law hasn't excluded us all should worry about it. Therefore, it does not say anywhere, or it is not specified that only local self-government units with a public fire department have the obligation to ensure the protection of their area of fire, but must do so by all municipalities and cities. We note this and I think it is very important that from 94 to 2000 professional fire departments of the Republic of Croatia had an area of activity and responsibility in the entire territory of each county, while according to the current provisions of the Fire Department Act, the representative body of the unit of local self-government was established and they have an area of action on the territory of the founders. Due to the limited scope of action, a number of public fire departments have recently proposed to neighbouring municipalities to carry out fire activities in their area of compensation. In view of the lack of financial resources or perhaps because of their desire to become as independent as possible, the municipalities generally refuse to enter into any relationship with the public fire department, which is why it prevents them from carrying out fire activities on their territory or in the area of those neighbouring municipalities. As a result of this unit of local self-government without a public fire department, fire protection must be organised either through voluntary fire or through contractual relations. Some may find it really difficult to understand that such important activities as fire protection can function on a voluntary basis. That it is thus demonstrated by cities on the list and by a multiple number of municipalities that have a fire department established through a voluntary fire company. Since this is a model that works very well in our neighbouring countries, it should be supported rather than placed in a discriminatory position. Generally looking at large and economically strong countries co-finance fire protection, and small cities and municipalities like now and in the past must do so on their own. In this way they were brought into a financial difficult position. On the basis of these examples, it is evident that the Final Proposal for a law supplementing the Fire Department should be extended in a way that includes central voluntary fire companies. As a rule, a voluntary fire company in a local self-government unit established by a fire protection plan, as well as the public fire departments carry out the fire work set out in Article 1 of the Law. The Fire Department Act. I think that we really shouldn't wait for a new law, but that, with intervention and in this bill, we need these central, voluntary fire companies to put us in the same position as the public fire departments. ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28506	Mr. President, ladies and gentlemen, this is a proposal to amend the two articles of Law 116 and 119. In accordance with Article 116 of the Law, it is determined that the postgraduate study will be established and implemented according to the provisions of this law, no later than the current academic year 2004 and 2005. The current situation shows that most of the higher schools of this study did not comply with this provision of the law. The proposed amendment of this article would set a deadline for the establishment and organisation of postgraduate studies with a deadline for setting up undergraduate studies, and they should be organised in 2005 and 2006 to ensure sufficient time for respect of that deadline. As with similar cases of concern for nature protection is a civilizational reach and certainly its level is administratively proportional to the degree of development of a society. The legislative and legal tradition in the field of nature protection in Croatian spaces has existed for hundreds of years and recently, so on. of 1999 Croatia is particularly active in the field of international cooperation in the protection of nature and has joined international agreements in the field of nature protection that are in force or signed by those who are yet to enter into force. Thus, we are party to the following international agreements of the Convention on the Protection of World Cultural and Natural Heritage, the Convention on Wetlands, the Convention on the Protection of the Mediterranean Sea against Contaminants, the Convention on Biological Variety, the Convention on the Protection of Migration Species of Wildlife, the Convention on the Protection of European Wildlifes, the Convention on International Trade of Threatened Species, the Convention on European Areas and the Convention on the Access of Information, the participation of the public in the decision-making and access of justice in the environment. In the context of the approximation of the European Union, the aim of nature protection policy is to meet the preconditions for meeting the international obligations provided for by the European Union but not primarily for the sake of the European Union, but first and foremost for our own sake, because thank God Croatia still has a lot to protect. Because, compared to most European countries, Croatia stands out by the diversity and richness of environmental systems and habitats. Croatia is characterized by a high degree of conservation of nature. The anthropogenic habitats developed by human action are not dominant as is the case in many European countries. The diversity of native varieties and breeds, and their recording and preservation, represent a genetic container for the potential improvement of the properties of breeds. Some protected parts of the nature of the Republic of Croatia also enjoy international legal protection as extremely valuable natural areas such as National park Plitvice Lakes, Nature Parks Kopački rit and Lonjsko polje, ornithological reserve Black Youth and in UNESCO's international network of biosphere reserves are also the lower flow of Neretva and Velebit mountains. The total area of all protected parts of nature in the Republic of Croatia is 9.83% of the national territory and by implementation of the Spatial Planning Programme and the strategy and action plan for the protection of biological and landscape diversity i.e. With the announcement of new nature parks, around 14% of state territory will be protected, which will be set for the Republic of Croatia in the European countries with temporary protection of nature. In addition, about 780 species of plants, mushrooms and animals as well as all wild species in national parks, rough and special reserves and all cave fauna are protected in the Republic of Croatia. Croatia ranks in the first few European Union countries, given the rich biodiversity but also at the bottom at the stage of flora, microflores and fauna research, which speaks of the necessity of establishing and developing a systematic research of imonitoring. Furthermore, in European terms, the state of the forest in the Republic of Croatia is well and even 95% of the forest according to some data has a natural composition and forest surfaces have not been reduced for a very long time. Also, given its natural characteristics, the area of debris represents an exceptional value not only in European but also in global proportions. In the Republic of Croatia, vast natural swamp areas in river valleys are preserved, which is a huge value of biological and landscape diversity, especially at Western and Central Europe level. The Croatian coast, coastal mountains and islands stand out with many plant and animal endens and are particularly sensitive ecological units. My colleagues and colleagues, this few data discusses the richness and diversity of nature in the Republic of Croatia and relatively good conservation at European level is due primarily to economic situation and similar causes in the past. However, there is still no way to talk about systematic care for the overall biodiversity and state of nature. That is why a precise and effective legislative regulation is needed to protect and protect what we still have and, as I said in the introduction, according to the information I have provided unlike others, we have much more to protect. The law we are discussing today regulates the issue of protection and preservation of protected natural values, the process of declaring protected natural values, the prevention of the area, the register of protected natural values, the implementation of protection and conservation of nature, the protection of environmental systems and networks, the incentive measures for conservation, the management of protected areas, the protection and conservation of wild species, the keeping of breeding and trade in wild species, the restriction on legal traffic, the use of natural goods, the use of protection measures, the access to information, the promotion of education and education in the protection of nature, as well as the supervision and criminal provisions. This Proposal for a Law addresses all the issues raised by international agreements, in particular the Convention on Biodiversity, and the Directive, the regulations and protocols of the European Union. It is therefore the level or standards which apply in Europe, and the safeguard measures prescribed will ensure the conditions for the reasonable use of natural resources on the principle of sustainability and for the benefit of future generations. Furthermore, in the proposal for a new law, today's one, which we are discussing today, there are no provisions concerning GMOs, genetically modified organisms. In the existing or applicable law, Article 49 regulates this problem. This problem will be regulated by a completely new law which, given the sensitivity of the topic, should be supported. ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28562	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President. Mr. Secretary of State, representatives of the ministry, colleagues and colleagues! In front of us today is the first reading of the debate on the Nature Protection Act. It's a very important law that bears the label P.Z.E. and harmonising the legislation of the Republic of Croatia with the legislation of the European Union in this area. The legislative tradition in Croatia regarding nature protection is more than a hundred years long and we are among the first countries in Europe with a normative system to protect natural heritage. The first laws in Croatia were the Law on the Protection of Birds in 1893 and the Law on the Protection of Caves in 1900, which speaks enough about early awareness in Croatia regarding nature conservation. As far as protected areas in the Republic of Croatia are concerned, about 9% of the state area is protected and 14% of the territory of the Republic of Croatia is under some form of protection and regulated by the Nature Protection Act. Croatia in terms of natural sights is a highly rich and diverse country. This claim is also supported by the spatial layout of two main spatial categories of protection of the nature of national parks and nature parks. They are equally represented in the coastal, mountainous and Pannonian parts of Croatia. So on the Croatian coast we have national parks Brijuni, Kornata and Mljet and Nature Park Telascic. On the contact of the coastal and mountainous Croatia, we have national parks Risnjak, North Velebit and Paklenic and nature parks Velebit, Učka and Biokovo. In particular, the Krka National Park needs one of the most beautiful examples of karst hydrography in the world. Our first Plitvice Lakes National Park cannot be bypassed in the continental part of Croatia, our only national park on the world list of UNESCO natural heritage. In the Pannonian part of Croatia, we have no national park but therefore we have parks of nature Kopački rit, Lonjsko polje, Medvednica, Papuk, Žumberak and Samoborsko Gorje. In addition to existing nature parks, this law suggests that nature parks become Neretva, Elafiti islands, Lastovo, Mrežnica, Lika Plješivica, Bjelolasica, Zrmanja, Krupa, Kupa and Hrvatska Zagorje. The Nature Protection Act proposes a very important provision that foreign legal or natural persons cannot acquire property rights in protected areas unless the international contract provides otherwise. The owner of the property in the protected area which he intends to sell is obliged to offer first to the state, county or city of Zagreb and to indicate the prices and conditions of sale and the state, county or city of Zagreb are required to demonstrate the offer within 90 days of receiving the offer. If the offer is not accepted within the prescribed time limit, the owner may sell the property on the terms of the offer. These two provisions on the acquisition of ownership in such valuable and protected works of Our beautiful obstacle are the sale of the most valuable thing Croatia has and I particularly welcome this. Compared to most European countries, Croatia stands out for the diversity of the ecological system and habitats, the great wealth and diversity of plants, mushrooms and animals, but the number of known species is more than double the number of suspected species. This points to a low degree of research of Croatian flora, micro flora and fauna and the necessity of establishing and developing systematic research and monitoring of biodiversity. I also need to stick to the establishment of a quality system for the protection of biodiversity and natural diversity so that the protection of nature and the environment is given an important place in the development of society and in state management. About 780 species of plants, mushrooms and animals of all wild species in national parks, strict and special reserves and all cave fauna are protected in Croatia. The proposed Nature Protection Act provides for fines for offenders. For example, the highest penalty is 500 thousand to million kuna for an operation or act that can cause destruction or any other significant or permanent damage in an ecologically important area. I hope that this extremely important law will be in its full capacity and that there will be an expression when, apart from the legal regulatory consciousness of man, there is such a level that there is no alternative to protecting nature. We need to be responsible for nature, cherish it and preserve it for those who come after us.

ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28566	Mr. Vice President, Mr. Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues. Nature is defined as a significant part of the environment within the meaning of this Act. overall biological and landscape diversity. The Republic of Croatia provides special protection to its nature and natural values on the principles of best world practice. The legislative tradition in the Republic of Croatia in the area of nature protection has already lasted over 100 years. This places us in line of the first countries in Europe with a normative system of nature protection, especially in the context of national parks and other protected parts of nature that enjoy international legal protection and are listed in the UNESCO World Heritage List, for example. like Plitvice National Park and Velebit Nature Park. The Republic of Croatia has signed 9 international agreements in the area of nature protection, and this obliges us to act very correctly and systematically towards our nature and our national good. For those of us working on these jobs and taking care of them, it is especially important to the Convention on the Protection of World Cultural Heritage and Nature, which was brought in Paris in 1972. A few words only about our current Law that was passed in 2003 as far as I know it and how much I could evaluate it it contains a series of provisions that are ineligible because it prescribes the obligation to adopt 60 sublegal acts and we know that so far a very small number has been adopted. In addition, keep the provisions on genetic organisms for here, for which we will pass another law and exclude it from the provisions of this Law. And in the implementing provisions of jurisdiction between individual bodies, there is no clear distinction. Therefore, and that we do not have to comply with the European fluid, the conditions for a new law have been established. The bill promoter in assessing the state of protected natural values in the Republic of Croatia points out that, thanks to its position in several biogenetic regions, the divorce of relief, geological, pedological, hydrological and climatic situations, the diversity of environmental systems and rich landscape diversity are highlighted. Compared with the state of the ecological systems of other European Union countries, the Republic of Croatia is characterised by a high degree of conservation of nature. So far, around 10% of state territory has been protected through 8 categories of spatial protection and individual plant and animal species. A total of 481 spatial objects were protected and another 30 spatial objects were declared in the process. So a total of 14% of state territory will be protected. In addition to protected areas, 780 species of plants, mushrooms and animals and all wild species that dwell in national parks, strict and special reserves and all cave fauna are protected. The diversity in relief, soil, water, plant cover of the afternoon but also in economic and historical circumstances resulted in local traditions of space use, which contributed to the extremely rich landscape of the Republic of Croatia. The legislator points out that this Law regulates the system of protection and full conservation of nature and its values, as well as all issues referred to by the international Treaties and Directives of the European Union, and in particular the Convention on Biological Divergence from Rio in 1992, which outlines a new concept of nature protection to preserve overall and landscape diversity and ensure the sustainable use of natural goods. Specific laws such as the Laws on Forests, Waters, Agricultural Land, freshwater and marine fisheries bring certain issues of nature protection closer together. This is a roof law for them, and therefore certain provisions in these laws will have to be brought into line with this Law, of course, with everything relating to nature protection. In the end, let me give a few more, I consider useful remarks concerning a certain provision of this Law. I'd say next. One general. I think it's very important. The responsibility of the Nature Protection Board and the Institute for Nature Protection at the Ministry of Culture are not clearly divided and intertwined, and it would be appropriate to consider the possibility of unifying those two bodies within the Ministry of Culture and Nature Protection. I would further point out that Article 9 needs to be reformed, it is clear that the provisions of this Act do not apply in the case of natural and other disasters and in the case of aggression against the Republic of Croatia. So you should make an explicit note of that. This Regulation shall enter into force on the twentieth day following that of its publication in the Official Journal of the European Union. It would not be correct to keep other ministries and other competent authorities in the Republic of Croatia in order to carry out their tasks in order not to fulfil certain obligations prescribed by this Act. Article 46 of the rules on the management of natural resources in the karst area determines the possible impact of their use on the entire area of land. It is necessary to determine which authority determines that impact, it cannot remain an open question. In Article 52(2), it is necessary to determine who determines the biological minimum water, the use of water. Like water streams, big rivers and water on lakes. ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28568	Thank you very much, Mr. Vice President of the Parliament, colleagues and colleagues. I would only limit this debate to the part relating to one aspect of this Law, which is one of its smaller part that regulates the issue of ownership and availability of real estate in the area of protected natural values. In other parts of its part, this proposal for a law largely introduces clearer and more effective solutions for the protection and conservation of nature, which are on the trail of the basic provisions of the legal property of the European Union and of international contracts, but which is even more important on the good track for the protection of national interests in the maintenance of natural heritage. The proposal for this law in part of ownership does not, unfortunately, contribute to the reduction of the doubts and contradictions that have existed in Croatia for a long time. In terms of real estate for foreigners, we are all involved in Croatia, including politics and the public, in at least three conflicting interests. Respect and acceptance of the fundamental values and freedoms on which the European Union operates, the undeniable desire to preserve the natural and other values of the Croatian national space and the inalienable rights of private owners to freely dispose and decide on real estate in their possession. I am afraid that already the public debate on regulating the rights of foreigners to acquire real estate in Croatia has slowly gone into the most sensitive and then the most dangerous water feeding xenophobia. If this is the prevailing tone and direction of this debate, then we will find it difficult to convince anyone of our willingness to accept and understand the areas of freedom that are of value in the European Union. In preparation for the upcoming negotiations on Croatia's membership in the European Union, representatives of the government are already coming out with two equally incorrect thesis. One is that the previous government has tied their hands and made it impossible for them to negotiate successfully in the part of the regulation of acquisition of property for foreigners because, in the negotiations on a Stabilisation and Association Agreement with the SAA, it has adopted harmful provisions to abolish the conditions of reciprocity within 4 years of the entry into force of the SAA. The second is that despite the new government's negotiators, they will be more successful, they will fight for new exceptions, and even a permanent ban on obtaining property for foreigners in Croatia, as Malta agreed in its accession talks, for example. I was the main negotiator for the SAA, and I know well that in this contract with the European Union we have installed all those restrictions, exceptions that, in terms of the rights of foreigners in real estate, existed in our national legislation at the time. All these existing exemptions will not need to be abolished by letter of agreement with the European Union, but after 2008 we will need to examine their retention together with the European Union and, optionally, abolish it. Expecting that the European Union would accept in our bilateral SAA some additional exceptions that did not exist at the time of the negotiations in national legislation are simply not realistic. The same issue will be addressed by new negotiators. That is why it must be clear to them today that they are encouraging too high and unfounded public expectations that, without the previous amendment of our legal solutions, they will urge the European Union negotiators to accept additional exemptions for the acquisition of real estate in Croatia. Objections that we agreed to abolish the conditions of reciprocity within 4 years, so somewhere in late 2008. Do those who fear foreigners in Croatia really believe that the condition of reciprocity is the only fuse for preventing foreigners from acquiring real estate in Croatia. If someone's goal is to prevent and restrict foreigners from buying real estate in Croatia, then it can be done much easier by detimulating demand for Croatian real estate, other measures in the area of ownership relations, building conditions, spatial planning than by retaining the conditions of reciprocity. In addition, we are likely to conclude negotiations on full membership in the European Union by the end of 2008, so that real estate relations will be treated according to what will be the outcome of these negotiations, not what was well or poorly agreed in the SAA. Although I am personally much closer to solutions that seek to affirm freedom at the disposal of real estate, I would well-intentionedly advise anyone who wants a greater national share among real estate owners to try to achieve this goal while this can still be achieved through changes to national legislation rather than in negotiations with the European Union. I have not yet noticed that the Government has ever called for a joint statement hidden somewhere at the end of the SAA, which allows us to, regardless of the obligations set out in Article 60. We introduce proportionate and non-discriminatory restrictions on the acquisition of property based on the general interest or to freely edit the ownership system. So the former government in the SAA managed to maintain all valid exemptions in the acquisition of real estate from our legislation and, even more so, opened the space to introduce new restrictions on full membership by amending national legislation based on the general interest and the need to regulate the ownership system. However, another important fence must go along with this. We can change real estate legislation, but we must not discriminatoryly treat foreigners with these changes, which is also natural because we can find enough strong arguments and for additional legal restrictions and bans in acquisition and availability of real estate and to convince the negotiators of the European Union if we do not explain to them the benefits that Croatia but also the European Union will have from the preserved natural environment and natural beauty especially along the Adriatic. But there are no arguments that could convince anyone that the coasts and islands or national parks would be more endangered if they were devastated by foreigners than they would be if they were devastated by local citizens. For this reason, I do not see any reason why the Government, in Article 113 of the proposed Nature Protection Act, continues to retain the ban on the acquisition of property rights for foreign legal and natural persons in areas of protected natural values. Even more this ban is now being extended to, from the areas of national parks and nature parks and to other areas of protected natural values. Any extension of the exemption is not in line with the obligation to maintain the exemption of those existing ones from the SAA. I would like to stress that the proposed solutions to the law of the first instance, the restrictions on legal traffic and the procedure and conditions for issuing permits for space procedures can be sufficiently protected by the interest of preserving natural values. These solutions also prevent the sale of real estate in areas of protected natural values in which foreigners could take advantage of their current material advantage over poorer categories of Croatian citizens. Additional restrictions for foreigners except that they are inadmissible against the fundamental European values and practice of freedom of movement of persons and capital bring unnecessary doubt to our European partners about our real understanding and acceptance of these values and areas of freedom in which the European Union operates. So without the proposed Article 113, the entire Nature Protection Act would be given the necessary European dimension and no one could deliberately object to the day Ismo using all the instruments to install into national legislation all necessary to protect and preserve nature. I therefore suggest that this article be deleted or at least left in the text resulting from the applicable law to another reading. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-HR_2004-11-30-0.u28574	Dear Mr. Vice-President of the Croatian Parliament, distinguished members of the representatives of the Croatian government. Dear colleagues and fellow MPs. Today we are discussing a proposal for a law on the protection of nature proposed by the Government of the Republic of Croatia. Compared to the size of European countries, Croatia is highlighted by the explicit diversity of ecological systems, and habitats, which are reflected in the great wealth and diversity of plant and animal species, and subtypes. The value of this diversity in European scales and this richness of nature are primarily the result of Croatia's position in relation to the biographic regions of relief and the impact of geological hydrological and climate opportunities. If we compare the ratio of the number of known species within well-examined groups, and the area of Croatia compared to the same data in other countries in Europe only then will we understand the value of this diversity. In Croatia, about 34 ethnic species of all plant and animal species are known, and there are estimated to be about 91 thousand in total. On the basis of these data, Croatia ranks among the few first countries in Europe, given the richness of biodiversity. What we can boast about compared to the state of the ecological systems of most Europe and European countries, Croatia is opting for a high degree of conservation of nature. What we are not satisfied with is that the number of known species in Croatia is more than twice as small as the number of suspected species. The diversity in soil, relief, water, plant cover, climate with different local traditions contributed to the exceptional wealth of the landscape diversity of Croatia in the European frameworks. Under the protection of biodiversity, we mean the recording and preservation of the common varieties of cultured plants and breeds of domestic animals, which represent a significant national and cultural heritage in which the cultivation of which the efforts and knowledge of many generations in Croatia has been made. Protected areas are the backbone of the overall protection of the plant and animal world, which are a sanctuary and containers of biological diversity. It is important to note that some parts of the protected nature in the Republic of Croatia enjoy international legal protection as an extremely valuable natural space. In the Republic of Croatia, 9.8% of the area of all protected parts of nature is total. The forest areas in Croatia have been well preserved and they have not been reduced in the last 100 years, which is very good for Croatia. In Croatia, natural wetlands are well preserved, in river valleys not only important for Croatia but also for Europe, so they should be given priority in nature protection and the national programme for their preservation and management. The exceptional value and high degree of conservation not only in European but also in global proportions is certainly an area of debris, which covers about 54% of Croatia. It has always been a problem how in protected areas to reconcile the co-existence of nature and human needs and economic needs, making sure that by protecting nature, we do not evict people from these areas. Nature conservation must not always be just a cost, contrary to nature parks and protected areas, tourism and entrepreneurship can be developed, and there are certainly Plitvice Lakes, Paklenica Canyon and others. Finally, I would say that the legislative tradition in the field of nature protection in Croatia has been going on for hundreds of years, and our strategic goal is to meet the necessary preconditions for meeting the international obligations laid down by European Union regulations. Thank you.
